The mark rules (first that fits):
1. CASE DISPUTED: the situation itself, read in ordinary terms before any theory, decides a point against the fixed verdict.
2. DISAGREE: on at least one point, the theory's verdict reaches a different finding from the fixed verdict.
3. SPLIT: on at least one point, the theory's sentences support two readings that reach different findings, and the text leaves the choice between them open.
4. SILENT: on at least one point, the theory leaves the finding open, because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it.
5. AGREE: on every point, the theory's verdict reaches the same finding as the fixed verdict.

Note my blind readings' fields (FINDINGS, QUOTE, OPEN, SPLIT). The blind mark derives from these.

Ugh. OK, I need to fix an interpretation of SPLIT vs SILENT and stick to it. Let me re-read the definitions once more very carefully:

"3. **SPLIT**: on at least one point, the theory's sentences support two readings that reach different findings, and the text leaves the choice between them open."

"4. **SILENT**: on at least one point, the theory leaves the finding open, because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it."

SPLIT: "the theory's sentences support two readings" - two readings of the theory's sentences. Readings = interpretations of what the theory says. If the theory's text is ambiguous (two sentences in tension, or a sentence admitting two construals) such that one construal gives finding F and another gives finding F', and the text doesn't resolve which, that's SPLIT.

SILENT: the theory has one determinate reading but it requires an input (declared index/input) the case doesn't supply, or the theory's sentences simply don't decide.

So for O1: the theory's sentences on tables - "A table of observed answers... fails (F1)" and "A table that genuinely encodes... is an account" - are these two readings of the theory's sentences? No - they're two branches of a consistent criterion, and which branch applies depends on Bruno's rule's nature (unstated). That's SILENT.

Argh. OK, I need to nail down the intended distinction between SPLIT and SILENT. Let me re-read the definitions one more time:

"3. **SPLIT**: on at least one point, the theory's sentences support two readings that reach different findings, and the text leaves the choice between them open."

"4. **SILENT**: on at least one point, the theory leaves the finding open, because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it."

SILENT's first clause: "it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated" - THE THEORY takes the deciding matter as an input. E.g., the theory says "given obligations O and P..." and the case doesn't state O and P -> the theory takes them as inputs -> SILENT. Or "given the declared boundary" -> case doesn't state -> SILENT. This is about the theory's declared inputs and indices!

SILENT's second clause: "its sentences stop short of deciding it" - the theory's sentences don't reach the point (e.g., "supplies no rule that certifies it").

SPLIT: "the theory's sentences support two readings that reach different findings" - the sentences themselves are ambiguous/two-fold. E.g., Part IV "a carrier keeps its provenance when access is lost" vs Part IX "missing evidence stays missing" - two sentences supporting "hole in account" and "no hole in account."

Hmm OK so the distinction: SPLIT = the theory has TWO voices (two readings of its sentences). SILENT = the theory has one voice but needs an input (declared or factual?) or stops short.

Hmm. Hmm, hmm. OK, let me re-read the ON QUOTES spec ONE more time: "ON QUOTES: <SUPPORTS, PARTLY or FAILS>, <one sentence: whether the sentences it quotes give the finding it says they give>".

"whether the sentences it quotes give the finding it says they give" - OK here's another parse: "the finding it says they give" = the finding that the reading under audit says [the quoted sentences] give. The reading under audit says [these sentences] give [this finding]. So: do the sentences give the finding claimed for them? Now, "the finding" (singular) = the row's finding = presumably the finding at issue (the verdict's conclusion on the case). So: do the quoted sentences support the verdict's conclusion? SUPPORTS = fully; PARTLY = partially (the conclusion is only partly carried); FAILS = no.

Under this, coverage of the conclusion matters. For rows where the conclusion (POINT: "ALL" or a specific point) is fully carried by the quotes -> SUPPORTS. Where partially -> PARTLY.
